A Fast-Convergence Resolution of the Stochastic Eigenproblem Using Halley’s Method and the Spectral-Chaos Approach
Hugo Esquivel, Kabir Oluwatobi Idowu, Guang Lin

Abstract
. Solving stochastic eigenvalue problems has long been essential for informed decision-making, advancing scientific knowledge, and ensuring the reliability of engineering designs and applications. This paper underscores the need to continue enhancing existing numerical methods for solving the stochastic eigenproblem in order to improve convergence rates, computational efficiency, and robustness. Specifically, we propose a novel spectral-chaos method for solving the stochastic (linear) eigenvalue problem, employing Halley’s method as the root-finding algorithm to leverage its cubic convergence properties. Our method achieves maximal convergence in solving stochastic eigenvalue problems since its rate cannot be further improved using a higher-order Householder method due to the quadratic nature of the resulting system of equations. Additionally, due to the complexity of the resulting system of equations, a tensorial approach was developed to tackle the challenges associated with the dimensional multiplicity of the stochastic eigenvalue problem, without which the solution would have been intractable. The method is derived rigorously, with a detailed error analysis that highlights the benefit of using our approach when the eigenvector components are nearly known, the computational cost of the method is also rigorously presented, and an illustrative example is provided to demonstrate the implementation of the method. Subsequently, a case study is demoed to analyze the results and validate the advantages of using Halley’s method over Newton’s method and Monte Carlo simulations.



1.
Introduction

The complexity of stochastic processes and their associated eigenvalue problems presents ongoing challenges in computational mathematics and applied sciences
[
18
,
32
,
16
]
.
Solving these eigenproblems is critical for applications in quantum physics, mathematics, engineering, and financial modeling, where stochastic models effectively represent inherent uncertainties
[
15
,
25
]
.
However, conventional methods often face limitations in effectiveness and accuracy, particularly when applied to complex high-dimensional systems
[
34
,
2
]
.
These challenges underscore the need for robust and efficient methods to address the stochastic eigenvalue problem in a manner that balances computational complexity with solution accuracy.

In this paper, we study the eigenspaces associated with stochastically governed linear dynamical models.
These models act as a substitute for the observed random spatial variations in the mechanical representation of the system
[
29
]
.
Such representations are crucial for capturing the probabilistic behavior of dynamical systems under uncertainty, particularly in engineering and physical sciences, where precise predictions of modal quantities are essential.
The resulting stochastic eigenvalue problem is critical for understanding dynamical systems as a whole
[
1
,
41
]
, as statistical measures of modal quantities have a substantial impact on the expected performance of these systems.
For example, in structural engineering, stochastic eigenvalue analyses guide the design of structures to ensure the stability and reliability of the system under uncertain loading conditions
[
3
,
5
,
28
]
.
In this case, stochastic reduced order models are often more successful because they effectively represent the observed variability of the system
[
37
,
19
,
43
]
.
Historically, these problems have been addressed using low-order Taylor expansions with perturbation methods or by analyzing statistical properties using low-order statistics
[
39
,
10
]
.
However, these approaches often fail to capture higher-order interactions, especially in systems with significant nonlinearity or higher-dimensional randomness
[
22
,
36
]
.

In contemporary science and engineering, random matrices are increasingly used as random perturbations of finite-dimensional operators to model uncertainty
[
13
,
35
,
42
]
.
Unlike matrices derived from finite-dimensional representations of partial differential operators, these random matrices often allow closed-form expressions of the statistical moments and probability density functions of their eigenvalues and eigenvectors in specific scenarios
[
12
,
7
]
.
However, the practical applicability of such methods is limited, particularly for large-scale systems or those characterized by complex parameter uncertainties.
Conversely, the matrices discussed in this paper arise from finite-dimensional approximations of continuous systems, where randomness stems from uncertainties in the system’s parameters.
For these systems, closed-form solutions to the random eigenvalue problem are typically unavailable, necessitating alternative strategies such as statistical sampling
[
1
,
44
]
, perturbation techniques
[
23
,
9
]
, or polynomial chaos representations combined with Galerkin projections
[
14
,
45
,
6
]
.
Although statistical sampling-based methods are conceptually straightforward, they are often computationally demanding and exhibit poor scalability with increasing system complexity
[
40
,
26
]
.

Halley’s method, known for its cubic convergence rate in root-finding problems, offers a promising approach to achieving reliable solutions
[
8
,
17
,
11
]
.
This superlinear convergence property makes it particularly suitable for solving nonlinear systems that exhibit rapid changes in solution behavior
[
4
,
24
]
.
However, its application to stochastic eigenproblems remains underexplored.
For example,
[
21
,
27
]
used polynomial chaos expansions to transform the stochastic problem into a deterministic one, thus allowing the computation of eigenvalues as functions of random variables.
Although effective, the quadratic rate of convergence inherent in these methods imposes limitations on computational efficiency, particularly for problems with high degrees of randomness or complex eigenvalue spectra.
Furthermore, reliance on deterministic transformations can lead to loss of precision in capturing intricate stochastic variations, especially in cases involving non-Gaussian randomness or multimodal distributions
[
33
,
20
]
.

This paper addresses these challenges by introducing an innovative application of Halley’s method within the spectral-chaos framework for solving stochastic eigenvalue problems.
Halley’s method, also known as the second-order Householder method, provides a cubic convergence rate—a significant improvement over the quadratic rate offered by Newton-based methods traditionally employed in polynomial chaos expansions
[
30
,
38
,
31
]
.
By reformulating the stochastic eigenvalue problem to enable the application of Halley’s method in a multidimensional random-spatial setting, the proposed approach achieves a polynomial-order enhancement in convergence rate and the ability to accelerate when the eigenvector components are nearly known.
This innovation preserves the strengths of polynomial chaos while substantially reducing computational effort and increasing precision.
Additionally, the introduction of a tensorial form of the stochastic operator facilitates the efficient manipulation of the random-spatial space, making this approach particularly effective for addressing the dimensional multiplicity of the stochastic eigenvalue problem.

Beyond its theoretical contributions, the proposed method offers practical benefits for computational efficiency and accuracy.
For example, the improved convergence rate significantly reduces the number of iterations required to achieve high precision, directly addressing the computational bottlenecks of existing methods.
Moreover, the ability to capture higher-order stochastic interactions makes our improved spectral-chaos approach suitable for a broader range of problems, including those involving nonstandard distributions or parameter uncertainties.

The following sections delve into the details of Halley’s method for solving stochastic (linear) eigenvalue problems and its implications for linear dynamical systems.
Here is a brief outline of the contents of each section.
Section
2
outlines the problem statement.
Section
3
introduces the spectral-chaos solution framework.
Section
4
explores the probability moments of the eigenpairs.
Section
5
presents the numerical scheme used to solve the resulting system of quadratic equations with Halley’s method.
Section
6
gives a detailed error analysis for the proposed method.
Section
7
provides the computational cost of the proposed method.
Section
8
demonstrates the method through an illustrative example.
Section
9
demos a case study to validate the robustness and efficiency of the proposed approach.
Finally, Section
10
offers concluding remarks.

2.
Problem statement

Before stating the problem, it is necessary to establish a series of definitions.

Let
(\Theta,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\nu)
be a
probability space
, where
\Theta
is the sample space (the set of all possible outcomes),
\boldsymbol{\Theta}\subset 2^{\Theta}
is the
\sigma
-algebra on
\Theta
(the collection of events), and
\nu:\boldsymbol{\Theta}\to[0,1]
is the probability measure on
\boldsymbol{\Theta}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}},\mu)
be the
random space
related to
(\Theta,\boldsymbol{\Theta},\nu)
via the measurable function
\zeta:(\Theta,\boldsymbol{\Theta})\to(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}})
defined by
\zeta=\zeta(\omega)
, where
\mathfrak{Z}=\zeta(\Theta)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the random domain (that is, the
\mathbb{R}^{d}
representation of
\Theta
),
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}}=\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\cap\mathfrak{Z}
is the
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathfrak{Z}
,
\mu:\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}}\to[0,1]
is the probability measure on
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}}
defined by the pushforward of
\nu
by
\zeta
(that is,
\mu=\zeta_{*}(\nu)
),
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
d
represents the dimensionality of the random space.

Let
(\mathfrak{E},\boldsymbol{\eta})
be the
spatial space
, where
\mathfrak{E}=\mathbb{R}^{R}
is the spatial domain,
R
denotes the dimensionality of the space, and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
is a Riemannian metric tensor (as specified below).
With this metric tensor,
\mathfrak{E}
is a Riemannian manifold.

Let
{Z}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}},\mu;\mathbb{R})
be a Lebesgue square-integrable space equipped with its standard inner product

\langle\cdot\,,\cdot\rangle:{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad(f,g)\mapsto\langle f,g\rangle=\int fg\,\mathrm{d}\mu.

This space is referred to as the
random function space
, and it is a Hilbert space.

Finally, let
\mathrm{L}(n,\mathbb{R})
be the space of linear maps from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathrm{Sym}(n,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathrm{L}(n,\mathbb{R})
the space of symmetric linear maps from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathrm{L}(n,\mathbb{R})
the space of linear isomorphisms from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The problem is to find the
n
-th random eigenpair
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\upphi}):\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathbb{R}^{R+1}
with
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,R\}
, such that (
\mu
-a.e.)

(1a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}\boldsymbol{\upphi}=\lambda\boldsymbol{\upphi}

on
\mathfrak{Z}

(1b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\upphi}\boldsymbol{\cdot}\boldsymbol{\upphi}=1

\displaystyle\quad\text{on $\mathfrak{Z}$},

where
\mathbf{K}:\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathrm{Sym}(R,\mathbb{R})
is a random, real symmetric matrix of size
R
,
\lambda:\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is the
n
-th random eigenvalue,
\boldsymbol{\upphi}:\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathfrak{E}
is the
n
-th random eigenvector, and
\,\boldsymbol{\cdot}\,:\mathfrak{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the dot product in linear algebra.
In
[
21
]
it was shown that if all entries of
\mathbf{K}
are in
{Z}
, so is
\lambda
, and if Eq. (
1b
) is considered to hold
\mu
-a.e., it is because all entries of
\boldsymbol{\upphi}
are in
{Z}
.

More generally, Eq. (
1
) can be expressed in tensor form as (
\mu
-a.e.)

(2a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)[\,\cdot\,,\boldsymbol{\phi}(\zeta)]=\lambda(\zeta)\,\boldsymbol{\phi}(\zeta)\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathfrak{Z}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}\phi^{v}=\lambda\phi^{u}

(2b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\eta}[\boldsymbol{\phi}(\zeta),\boldsymbol{\phi}(\zeta)]=1\quad\forall\zeta\in\mathfrak{Z}

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\eta^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.35597pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8257pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{uv}}}\phi^{u}\phi^{v}=1,

where
\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{e}_{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}:\mathfrak{E}^{*}\times\mathfrak{E}\to\mathbb{R}
is a spatial
(1,1)
-tensor with the property that its flat,
\boldsymbol{K}^{\flat}(\zeta)
, is symmetric,
\boldsymbol{\phi}(\zeta)=\phi^{u}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{e}_{u}
is a spatial vector,
\mathbf{e}_{u}\in\mathfrak{E}
is the standard unit vector in the
u
-th dimension,
\mathbf{e}^{v}:\mathfrak{E}\to\mathbb{R}
is the standard covector in the
v
-th dimension,
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\eta_{uv}\,\mathbf{e}^{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}:\mathfrak{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Riemannian metric tensor endowed on
\mathfrak{E}
(typically, the Euclidean metric tensor),
\mathfrak{E}^{*}
is the dual space of
\mathfrak{E}
with
\mathbf{e}^{v}[\mathbf{e}_{u}]=\delta^{v}_{u}
(the Kronecker delta),
K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}},\phi^{u},\lambda:\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
are random functions in
{Z}
, and
u,v\in\{1,2,\ldots,R\}
.
As is customary in multi-linear algebra, a summation is always implied over repeated indices—this is known as the Einstein summation convention.

Remark 1
.

A more compelling way to define the tensor field
\boldsymbol{K}
described above is by defining it as a
{Z}
section of
\mathrm{T}^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}(\mathbf{Z}):=\mathbf{Z}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{*}
, where
\mathbf{Z}:=\mathfrak{Z}\times\mathbb{R}^{R}\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathfrak{Z}
is the trivial vector bundle of rank
R
over
\mathfrak{Z}
.
This can be accomplished through a two-step process as follows.
First, let:

\boldsymbol{K}:\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathrm{T^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}}(\mathbf{Z})\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad\zeta\mapsto\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{e}_{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}\in\mathrm{T}^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}(\mathbf{Z}_{\zeta})\quad\text{with}\quad K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}\in{Z},

where
\mathbf{Z}_{\zeta}=\pi^{-1}(\{\zeta\})=\{\zeta\}\times\mathbb{R}^{R}
is the fiber over
\zeta\in\mathfrak{Z}
, which can be identified with
\mathfrak{E}=\mathbb{R}^{R}
.
Then, define:

\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{e}_{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}:(\mathbf{Z}_{\zeta})^{*}\times\mathbf{Z}_{\zeta}\to\mathbb{R}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad(\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbf{w})\mapsto\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)[\boldsymbol{\alpha},\mathbf{w}]=\alpha_{u}w^{v}\,K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\zeta),

where
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=\alpha_{u}\mathbf{e}^{u}
, and
\mathbf{w}=w^{v}\mathbf{e}_{v}
.
Put differently,
\boldsymbol{K}
is a
{Z}
-tensor field of order
(1,1)
on
\mathfrak{Z}
, assigning to each point
\zeta\in\mathfrak{Z}
an element
\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)
of
\mathrm{T}^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}(\mathbf{Z}_{\zeta})\cong\mathfrak{E}\otimes\mathfrak{E}^{*}
.
However, for the sake of simplicity, we opted for the more straightforward, albeit more decorated, definition presented earlier.
Likewise,
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is a
{Z}
section of
\mathbf{Z}
(or a
{Z}
-vector field on
\mathfrak{Z}
), and
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\eta}[\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,]
can be reinterpreted as a
{C}
section of
\mathrm{T}^{{{0}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{2}}}(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{Z}^{*}\otimes\mathbf{Z}^{*}
, with
{C}\subset{Z}
symbolizing the space of real-valued constant functions on
\mathfrak{Z}
; hence, admitting the form of a tensor field,
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\boldsymbol{\eta}(\cdot)[\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,]
.

Remark 2
.

Because by definition
\mathbf{K}(\zeta)\in\mathrm{Sym}(R,\mathbb{R})
, we require that the flat of
\boldsymbol{K}(\zeta)
:

\boldsymbol{K}^{\flat}(\zeta)=\eta_{uw}K^{{{w}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.9734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.9734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.63985pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59988pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\zeta)\,\mathbf{e}^{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}:\mathfrak{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

is symmetric as well; i.e., we require that
\boldsymbol{K}^{\flat}(\zeta)[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]=\boldsymbol{K}^{\flat}(\zeta)[\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}]
for all
\zeta\in\mathfrak{Z}
and
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathfrak{E}
.

3.
Spectral solution of eigenproblem

Let
(\varphi_{j}:(\mathfrak{Z},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{Z}})\to(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{R}}))_{j=0}^{\infty}
be an ordered orthogonal basis in
{Z}
with
\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
.
If
f
is an element of
{Z}=\mathrm{span}(\varphi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
, then

(3)

f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}f^{j}\varphi_{j}\equiv f^{j}\varphi_{j},

where
f^{j}\in\mathbb{R}
is the
j
-th component of
f
.
The orthogonality property of the basis implies that

(4)

\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\rangle=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle\,\delta^{i}_{j}.

Moreover, define
{Z}^{\prime}
to be the dual space of
{Z}
spanned by the continuous linear functionals
(\varphi^{i}:{Z}\to\mathbb{R})_{i=0}^{\infty}
given by

(5)

\varphi^{i}[f]:=[\varphi^{i},f]=\frac{\langle\varphi_{i},f\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle}=f^{j}\frac{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle}=f^{j}\delta^{i}_{j}=f^{i},

where
[\,\cdot\,,\cdot\,]:{Z}^{\prime}\times{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is the natural pairing of
{Z}
and
{Z}^{\prime}
given by the first equality.
The second equality follows from Eq. (
3
), and the third equality from Eq. (
4
).
Thus, when
\varphi^{i}
acts on
f\in{Z}
, it outputs the
i
-th component of
f
.
Another result from the definition is that
\varphi^{i}[\varphi_{j}]=\delta^{i}_{j}
.

Due to the assumption that both
\lambda
and
\phi^{v}
are in
{Z}
, we have

(6a)

\displaystyle\lambda=\lambda^{j}\varphi_{j}:{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\zeta\mapsto\lambda(\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda^{j}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta)\equiv\lambda^{j}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta)

(6b)

\displaystyle\phi^{v}=\phi^{vj}\varphi_{j}:{Z}\to\mathbb{R}\quad

\displaystyle:\Leftrightarrow\quad\zeta\mapsto\phi^{v}(\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\phi^{vj}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta)\equiv\phi^{vj}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta).

Substituting Eq. (
6
) into Eq. (
2
) gives

(7a)

\displaystyle K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}(\phi^{vj}\varphi_{j})=(\lambda^{k}\varphi_{k})(\phi^{uj}\varphi_{j})

(7b)

\displaystyle\eta_{uv}(\phi^{uj}\varphi_{j})(\phi^{vk}\varphi_{k})=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{0},

where
\varphi_{0}\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
because
\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
by definition.

Projecting Eq. (
7
) onto
{Z}
yields

(8a)

\displaystyle\varphi^{i}[K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}\phi^{vj}\varphi_{j}]=\varphi^{i}[\lambda^{k}\phi^{uj}\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}]

(8b)

\displaystyle\varphi^{i}[\eta_{uv}\phi^{uj}\phi^{vk}\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}]=\varphi^{i}[\varphi_{0}\varphi_{0}],

which after simplification produces a system of infinitely many quadratic equations:

(9a)

\displaystyle K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\phi^{vj}=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\lambda^{k}\phi^{uj}

(9b)

\displaystyle I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\phi^{uj}\phi^{vk}=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{00}}}

with
K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}
and
I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}
given by

(10)

K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}=\frac{\langle\varphi_{i},K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}}}\varphi_{j}\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle}\quad\text{and}\quad I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}=\frac{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle}.

Remark 3
.

If the expressions in Eq. (
10
) do not yield real numbers, it means that the problem cannot be solved using the spectral approach.
This is because
{Z}
does not form an algebra.

Remark 4
.

Eq. (
10
) entails the existence of the random-spatial tensors:

(11a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\,\mathbf{e}_{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}\otimes\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi^{j}:\mathfrak{E}^{*}\times\mathfrak{E}\times{Z}^{\prime}\times{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

(11b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{I}}=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\,\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi^{j}\otimes\varphi^{k}:{Z}^{\prime}\times{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},

where
\boldsymbol{\tilde{I}}
is symmetric in the indices
j
and
k
, and the tensor

\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}^{\flat_{1}}=K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.35597pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8257pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{uv}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\,\mathbf{e}^{u}\otimes\mathbf{e}^{v}\otimes\varphi_{i}\otimes\varphi^{j}:\mathfrak{E}^{2}\times{Z}^{\prime}\times{Z}\to\mathbb{R}

is symmetric in the indices
u
and
v
because
\mathbf{K}
is a symmetric matrix by definition.
Since
\flat_{1}
denotes the flat of
\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}
with respect to its first slot, we have
K^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.97925pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.35597pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.8257pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{uv}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}=\eta_{uw}K^{{{w}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.9734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.9734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.63985pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59988pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}
.

4.
Probability moments of eigenpair

The key probability moments are the mean and variance of the eigenpair
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\phi})
.
If
\varrho
denotes the
k
-th coordinate of
(\lambda,\boldsymbol{\phi})
with
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,R+1\}
, then we have

(12)

\varrho(\zeta)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\varrho^{j}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta)\equiv\varrho^{j}\,\varphi_{j}(\zeta),

where the
j
-th component of
\varrho
is given by

\varrho^{j}=\varphi^{j}[\varrho]=\frac{\langle\varphi_{j},\varrho\rangle}{\langle\varphi_{j},\varphi_{j}\rangle}.

Moreover, let the standard metric tensor on
{Z}
be taken as

(13)

\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}=\Upsilon_{ij}\,\varphi^{i}\otimes\varphi^{j}:{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad(f,g)\mapsto\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}[f,g]=\Upsilon_{ij}f^{i}g^{j},

where
\Upsilon_{ij}=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\rangle
, and
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Remark 5
.

The definition on the right-hand side of Eq. (
13
) follows from standard conventions in multi-linear algebra.
An explicit derivation is given below:

\displaystyle(f,g)\mapsto\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}[f,g]

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{ij}\,\varphi^{i}[f]\,\varphi^{j}[g]=\Upsilon_{ij}\,\varphi^{i}[f^{k}\varphi_{k}]\,\varphi^{j}[g^{l}\varphi_{l}]=\Upsilon_{ij}f^{k}g^{l}\,\varphi^{i}[\varphi_{k}]\,\varphi^{j}[\varphi_{l}]

\displaystyle=\Upsilon_{ij}f^{k}g^{l}\delta^{i}_{k}\delta^{j}_{l}=\Upsilon_{kl}f^{k}g^{l}=\Upsilon_{ij}f^{i}g^{j}.

The fourth and fifth equalities follow from the fact that
\varphi^{i}[\varphi_{j}]=\delta^{i}_{j}
and that
\Upsilon_{ij}\delta^{i}_{k}\delta^{j}_{l}=\Upsilon_{kl}
after simplification, while the last equality follows from reindexing the dummy indices
k
and
l
to
i
and
j
, respectively.
The same procedure can be used to assert, for instance, that
\boldsymbol{\eta}[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]=\eta_{uv}x^{u}y^{v}
for all
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathfrak{E}
, given that
\mathbf{e}^{v}[\mathbf{e}_{u}]=\delta^{v}_{u}
by definition.

Then, thanks to the orthogonality property of the random basis, the definition of
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}
reduces to

(14)

\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}[f,g]=\Upsilon_{ij}f^{i}g^{j}=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\rangle\,f^{i}g^{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle\,\delta^{i}_{j}\,f^{i}g^{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{i}\rangle\,f^{i}g^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\Upsilon_{ii}f^{i}g^{i},

where the third equality follows from Eq. (
4
).

From Eqs. (
12
) and (
14
), we can deduce the following.
The mean of
\varrho
,
\mathbf{E}[\varrho]\in\mathbb{R}
, is simply its first component:

(15)

\mathbf{E}[\varrho]=\int\varrho\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\varrho^{j}\int\varphi_{j}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\varrho^{j}\langle\varphi_{0},\varphi_{j}\rangle=\Upsilon_{0j}\varrho^{j}=\varrho^{0},

while the variance of
\varrho
,
\mathrm{Var}[\varrho]\in\mathbb{R}
, is obtained by computing a specific series:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\varrho]

\displaystyle=\int(\varrho-\mathbf{E}[\varrho])^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int(\varrho^{j}\varphi_{j}-\varrho^{0})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\int(\varrho^{j}\varphi_{j}-\varrho^{0}\varphi_{0})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu

(16)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\varrho^{j}\varrho^{k}\int\varphi_{j}\varphi_{k}\,\mathrm{d}\mu=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\varrho^{j}\varrho^{k}\langle\varphi_{j},\varphi_{k}\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\Upsilon_{jj}\varrho^{j}\varrho^{j}.

5.
Numerical solution to the system of equations

We employ Halley’s method to numerically solve Eq. (
9
), thus taking advantage of its cubic convergence properties.
However, to ensure computational feasibility, we must first discretize the random function space
{Z}
.
To this end,
{Z}
is
p
-discretized below, without loss of generality.

Let
{Z}^{[P]}=\mathrm{span}(\varphi_{j})_{j=0}^{P}
be a finite subspace of
{Z}
with
P+1\in\mathbb{N}_{1}
representing the dimensionality of the subspace.
If
f
is an element of
{Z}
, then it can be represented in
{Z}^{[P]}
as

(17)

\sum_{j=0}^{P}f^{j}\varphi_{j}\equiv f^{j}\varphi_{j}.

Let
Q=(R+1)(P+1)
.
If
{Z}
is represented with
{Z}^{[P]}
, then Eq. (
9
) is a system of
Q
quadratic equations with
Q
unknowns.
The unknowns being the two points:

\lambda=(\lambda^{0},\ldots,\lambda^{i},\ldots,\lambda^{P})\in\mathbb{R}^{P+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi=(\phi^{10},\ldots,\phi^{ui},\ldots,\phi^{RP})\in\mathbb{R}^{R(P+1)}.

Remark 6
.

In this work,
\lambda
has two meanings: it can represent either the random function
\lambda
or the
(P+1)
-dimensional point
\lambda
.
Nonetheless, the intended meaning of
\lambda
should always be clear from the context.

Let
g^{ui},h^{i}:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
be the associated functions of Eq. (
9
):

(18a)

\displaystyle g^{ui}(x)=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\phi^{vj}-I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\lambda^{k}\phi^{uj}

\displaystyle=0

(18b)

\displaystyle h^{i}(x)=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\phi^{uj}\phi^{vk}-I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97226pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.9pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.5pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{00}}}

\displaystyle=0,

where

x=(\lambda,\phi)=(\lambda^{0},\ldots,\lambda^{i},\ldots,\lambda^{P},\phi^{10},\ldots,\phi^{ui},\ldots,\phi^{RP})\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}

with
u\in\{1,2,\ldots,R\}
and
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,P\}
.

To simplify notation, the left-hand side of Eq. (
18
) is redefined as

(19)

f=g\times h:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{Q}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad x\mapsto f(x)=(f^{1}(x),\ldots,f^{n}(x),\ldots,f^{Q}(x)),

where
f
is the cartesian product of
g
and
h
such that
f(x)=(g(x),h(x))
,
f^{n}:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
is the
n
-th coordinate function of
f
with
n\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
, and the functions
g
and
h
are given by

\displaystyle g:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{R(P+1)}

\displaystyle\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad x\mapsto g(x)=(g^{10}(x),\ldots,g^{ui}(x),\ldots,g^{RP}(x))

\displaystyle h:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{P+1}

\displaystyle\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad x\mapsto h(x)=(h^{0}(x),\ldots,h^{i}(x),\ldots,h^{P}(x)).

As a result, the system of equations to be solved hereafter is

(20)

f(x)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}\quad\text{which is equivalent to}\quad\{f^{n}(x)=0\}_{n=1}^{Q}.

Linearizing Eq. (
20
) about
x=\hat{x}
gives

(21)

f^{n}(\hat{x})+\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})=0,

where
\hat{x}
denotes the trial
x
, assumed to be sufficiently close to the solution, and
m\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
.
(A summation is implied on the index
m
.)

Multiplying both sides of Eq. (
21
) by

\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\quad\text{with $r\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}$},

we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}f^{n}(\hat{x})+\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}f^{n}(\hat{x})+\delta^{r}_{m}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})

\displaystyle=0

(22)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}f^{n}(\hat{x})+x^{r}-\hat{x}^{r}

\displaystyle=0.

(A summation is implied on the index
n
.) The second line follows from the chain rule:

\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}=\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}=\delta^{r}_{m}.

Solving Eq. (
5
) for
x^{r}-\hat{x}^{r}
yields

(23)

x^{r}-\hat{x}^{r}=-\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}f^{n}(\hat{x}).

Moreover, quadratizing Eq. (
20
) about
x=\hat{x}
gives

(24)

f^{n}(\hat{x})+\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f^{n}}{\partial x^{r}\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}(x^{r}-\hat{x}^{r})(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})=0,

where
m,r\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
.
(Summations are implied on the indices
m
and
r
.)

Now let
\tilde{\mathbb{R}}^{Q}
be some thickening of
\mathbb{R}^{Q}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2Q}
, and let
\mathcal{H}:\tilde{\mathbb{R}}^{Q}\to\mathrm{L}(Q,\mathbb{R})
be the extended Halleyian of
f
with its
(n,m)
-th entry at
(u,v)
defined by

(25)

\mathcal{H}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(u,v)=\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{u}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}f^{n}}{\partial x^{r}\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{u}v^{r},

where
n,m,r\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
.
(A summation is implied on the index
r
.)

Remark 7
.

More specifically, in this work, the thickening of
\mathbb{R}^{Q}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2Q}
is defined as

\tilde{\mathbb{R}}^{Q}=\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}\times\mathbb{R}^{Q}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{2Q}:\|(0,v)\|_{2}<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2Q},

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{2Q}
, and
\varepsilon>0
is a pre-specified (small) positive number.

Substituting
\hat{\mathcal{H}}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}:=\mathcal{H}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(\hat{x},\,x-\hat{x})
into Eq. (
24
) gives

(26)

f^{n}(\hat{x})+\hat{\mathcal{H}}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})=0.

Thus, solving Eq. (
26
) for
x^{m}
yields

(27)

x^{m}=\hat{x}^{m}-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{{{m}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{n}}}\,f^{n}(\hat{x}),

where
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
symbolizes the inverse of
\hat{\mathcal{H}}
, provided that
\mathcal{H}
is non-singular at
(\hat{x},\,x-\hat{x})
; that is, now we require that
\mathcal{H}(\hat{x},\,x-\hat{x})=\hat{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathrm{GL}(Q,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathrm{L}(Q,\mathbb{R})
.

However, one problem with Eq. (
27
) is that
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
depends upon
x
, the unknown in Eq. (
24
).
In Halley’s method this problem is resolved by defining the
r
-th coordinate of
x-\hat{x}
using Eq. (
23
).
Thus, by considering the function

q:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathbb{R}^{Q}\quad:\Leftrightarrow\quad u\mapsto q(u)=(q^{1}(u),\ldots,q^{r}(u),\ldots,q^{Q}(u))\quad\text{with}\quad q^{r}(u)=-\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{s}}\bigg|_{f(u)}f^{s}(u)\in\mathbb{R},

we can obtain the
(n,m)
-th entry of
H=\mathcal{H}(\,\cdot\,,q(\cdot)):\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathrm{L}(Q,\mathbb{R})
at
\hat{x}
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{H}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}=H^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(\hat{x})=\mathcal{H}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(\hat{x},q(\hat{x}))

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{s}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\frac{\partial^{2}f^{n}}{\partial x^{r}\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}f^{s}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial x^{r}}{\partial f^{s}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{r}}\left(\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\right)\bigg|_{\hat{x}}f^{s}(\hat{x})

(28)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial f^{s}}\left(\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\right)\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}f^{s}(\hat{x}),

where
H
symbolizes the Halleyian of
f
,
s\in\{1,2,\ldots,Q\}
, and the last line follows from the chain rule.

Remark 8
.

Note that
q
is a function that assigns to each point in
\mathbb{R}^{Q}
a point in
\mathbb{O}^{Q}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}:\|v\|_{2}<\varepsilon\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{Q}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
is the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{Q}
, and
\varepsilon
is the positive number pre-specified in
\tilde{\mathbb{R}}^{Q}=\mathbb{R}^{Q}\times\mathbb{O}^{Q}
.

Hence, replacing
\hat{\mathcal{H}}
with
\hat{H}
in Eq. (
26
) results in

(29)

f^{n}(\hat{x})+\hat{H}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(x^{m}-\hat{x}^{m})=0,

and so, by solving for
x^{m}
, we get the
m
-th coordinate trial of
x
:

(30)

x^{m}=\hat{x}^{m}-\hat{L}^{{{m}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{n}}}\,f^{n}(\hat{x}),

where
\hat{L}
represents the inverse of
\hat{H}
, assuming
H(\hat{x})=\hat{H}\in\mathrm{GL}(Q,\mathbb{R})
.

Supposing
f
is locally a diffeomorphism at
\hat{x}
, let
F:\mathbb{R}^{Q}\to\mathrm{GL}(Q,\mathbb{R})
and
X=F^{-1}
be the Jacobian and the Jacobian inverse of
f
, respectively, such that their entries at
\hat{x}
and
f(\hat{x})
are:

(31)

\hat{F}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}=F^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}(\hat{x})=\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{X}^{{{m}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{n}}}=X^{{{m}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{n}}}(f(\hat{x}))=\frac{\partial x^{m}}{\partial f^{n}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}.

Remark 9
.

Observe that as
\hat{x}
approaches the solution, i.e., as
f(\hat{x})\to 0
,
H(\hat{x})
tends to
F(\hat{x})
.
Indeed,

f(\hat{x})\to 0\in\mathbb{R}^{Q}\quad\Rightarrow\quad q(\hat{x})\to 0\in\mathbb{O}^{Q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{Q}\quad\Rightarrow\quad H(\hat{x})\to\mathcal{H}(\hat{x},0)=F(\hat{x}).

Therefore, there is a moment at which switching from
H
to
F
becomes computationally advantageous.
Nevertheless, such an approach was not implemented in this work to avoid the need for defining a stopping condition within the numerical scheme.

Eq. (
31
) can also be expressed in matrix form as

(32)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{F}}=[\hat{F}^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}]=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{F}^{{{ui}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49315pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}&\hat{F}^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}\\[4.30554pt]
\hat{F}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}&\hat{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\mathbf{X}}=[\hat{X}^{{{m}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{n}}}]=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{X}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}&\hat{X}^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displ \hat{X}^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}&\hat{X}^{{{\alpha\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.73317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.9061pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.21864pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}\end{bmatrix},

where
n\in(ui,i)
and
m\in(\beta,\alpha\beta)
with
u,\alpha\in\{1,2,\ldots,R\}
and
i,\beta\in\{0,1,\ldots,P\}
.

The entries of
\hat{\mathbf{F}}
are determined by differentiating Eq. (
18
) at
\hat{x}
:

(33a)

\hat{F}^{{{ui}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49315pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}:=\frac{\partial \displaystyle\hat{F}^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}:=\frac{\partial g^{ui}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}

\displaystyle=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\frac{\partial\phi^{vj}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}-I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\lambda^{k}\frac{\partial\phi^{uj}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}

\displaystyle=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.25119pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55093pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{v}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}\delta^{v}_{\alpha}\delta^{j}_{\beta}-I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\hat{\lambda}^{k}\delta^{u}_{\alpha}\delta^{j}_{\beta}=K^{{{u}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.72806pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80504pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.0036pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}-I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\make j}}}\hat{\lambda}^{j}\delta^{u}_{\alpha}.

(33c)

\hat{F}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}:=\frac{\partial h^{i}}{\partial\lambda^{\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}=0.

\displaystyle\hat{F}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}:=\frac{\partial h^{i}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}

\displaystyle=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\frac{\partial\phi^{uj}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\phi^{vk}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}+I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\phi^{uj}\frac{\partial\phi^{vk}}{\partial\phi^{\alpha\beta}}\bigg|_{\hat{x}}

\displaystyle=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\delta^{u}_{\alpha}\delta^{j}_{\beta}\hat{\phi}^{vk}+I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.13373pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.00354pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.57394pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{jk}}}\eta_{uv}\hat{\phi}^{uj}\delta^{v}_{\alpha}\delta^{k}_{\beta}=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.47676pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.91197pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta k}}}\eta_{\alpha v}\hat{\phi}^{vk}+I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.0699pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.62135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j\beta}}}\eta_{u\alpha}\hat{\phi}^{uj}

(33d)

\displaystyle=I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.0699pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.62135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta j}}}\eta_{\alpha u}\hat{\phi}^{uj}+I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.0699pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.62135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta j}}}\eta_{\alpha u}\hat{\phi}^{uj}=2I^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.24797pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.0699pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.62135pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta j}}}\eta_{\alpha u}\hat{\phi}^{uj}=-2\eta_{\alpha u}\hat{F}^{{{ui}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.55734pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.49315pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\beta}}}.

The entries of
\hat{\mathbf{X}}
are determined by taking the matrix inverse of
\hat{\mathbf{F}}
:

(34)

\hat{\mathbf{X}}=\hat{\mathbf{F}}^{-1}.

(35)

\frac{\partial}{\partial f^{s}}\left(\frac{\partial f^{n}}{\partial x^{m}}\right)\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}\equiv\frac{\partial F^{{{n}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.09612pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.30226pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.07304pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.94333pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.94115pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10081pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{m}}}}{\partial f^{s}}\bigg|_{f(\hat{x})}.

When expanded, this expression gives rise to eight distinct objects, whose explicit components are detailed in Appendix A.

can be expressed as
